I guess what brings me in is that I just feel… wound up, pretty much all the time. Like my body is constantly bracing for something bad to happen, even when there’s no obvious reason. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… definitely. It’s like my worry doesn’t stay in one place anymore. At first I think I was more focused on the condo and practical stuff around that, but now it kind of jumps around all day.

Like I’ll be wondering if my cousin is upset with me because of something I said, and I’ll replay the conversation in my head and try to figure out if her tone seemed different. Then I’ll be at work and suddenly it’s about whether I’m going to make some dumb mistake on a support call, or miss something obvious, or sound incompetent. And then later it turns into bigger life stuff, like how I’m ever going to save enough money, or move forward, or feel like I’m not falling behind everyone else.

It’s not always that there’s a clear problem happening right then. Sometimes there’s nothing specific, and my mind just… finds something to attach to. And once it does, it feels hard to let it go.

And then later it turns into bigger life stuff, like how I’m ever going to save enough money, or move forward, or feel like I’m not falling behind everyone else.\n\nIt’s not always that there’s a clear problem happening right then. Yes, especially the tension and that restless, wound-up feeling. It’s kind of the main reason I came in, honestly. I feel like my body is bracing for something bad to happen, even when there isn’t anything happening.

Like I’ll just be sitting at my desk at work, answering tickets or staring at a screen, and my shoulders are already up near my ears, or I realize I’m holding myself really rigid. And it’s not even always because something stressful just happened. It’s more like my body is already preparing for the next thing to go wrong.

It’s exhausting in a way, because I don’t feel like I get many moments where my body actually believes I’m safe or settled.

It’s like part of me is waiting for the next problem, the next bad thing, and I’m tired of feeling like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about different things in your life, like work, relationships, or other everyday issues, even when there isn’t a clear reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. It’s like my worry doesn’t stay in one place anymore. At first I think I was more focused on the condo and practical stuff around that, but now it kind of jumps around all day.\n\nLike I’ll be wondering if my cousin is upset with me because of something I said, and I’ll replay the conversation in my head and try to figure out if her tone seemed different. Then I’ll be at work and suddenly it’s about whether I’m going to make some dumb mistake on a support call, or miss something obvious, or sound incompetent. And then later it turns into bigger life stuff, like how I’m ever going to save enough money, or move forward, or feel like I’m not falling behind everyone else.\n\nIt’s not always that there’s a clear problem happening right then. Sometimes there’s nothing specific, and my mind just… finds something to attach to. And once it does, it feels hard to let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you often experience physical symptoms like restlessness, fatigue, or muscle tension when you’re feeling anxious?\n\nPatient: Yes, especially the tension and that restless, wound-up feeling. It’s kind of the main reason I came in, honestly. I feel like my body is bracing for something bad to happen, even when there isn’t anything happening.\n\nLike I’ll just be sitting at my desk at work, answering tickets or staring at a screen, and my shoulders are already up near my ears, or I realize I’m holding myself really rigid. And it’s not even always because something stressful just happened. It’s more like my body is already preparing for the next thing to go wrong.\n\nEven when I’m supposed to be relaxing, like at dance night, I’ll notice I’m still tense inside. I want to enjoy it and just be there, but part of me feels on alert, like I can’t fully let go. It’s exhausting in a way, because I don’t feel like I get many moments where my body actually believes I’m safe or settled.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever feel this anxious or tense even when you’re completely alone and there’s no immediate situation, person, or task causing stress?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 29 / computer_support_specialist / graduate\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Restlessness_Tension\nmanifestation: \"I came in because I feel wound up almost all the time, like my body is bracing for the next bad thing even when I'm just sitting at my desk or trying to enjoy dance night.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. Honestly, that’s part of what feels so frustrating about it, because it doesn’t always need a trigger. I can be completely by myself, with nothing actively happening, and I’ll still feel like my body is waiting for something bad to happen.

Like I’ll just be sitting at my desk, or at home, or even trying to do something that should be enjoyable, and there’s this wound-up feeling in me. My shoulders get tight, and I feel like I’m bracing, almost like I’m preparing for the next problem before I even know what it is.

And with dance night, too — even though I want to relax and enjoy it, I’ll notice I’m still tense inside. It’s not necessarily that someone said anything or that there’s some immediate task I’m stressed about. It’s more like my body hasn’t gotten the message that everything is okay. It just stays on alert.

It’s most days. I wish it were more like it came and went in a clear way, but it feels pretty constant lately.

I mean, it may get louder or quieter depending on what I’m doing, but the baseline is usually there — like I’m wound up, or my body is bracing for the next bad thing. Even if I’m just sitting at my desk, not in the middle of anything dramatic, I’ll notice I’m tight and tense, like I’m already preparing for something to go wrong.

And then even during things I actually want to enjoy, like dance night, it doesn’t fully switch off. I might be trying to have fun, but there’s still this tension underneath it, like I can’t completely relax. So… yeah, I’d say it’s there most days, not just once in a while.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. That’s actually one of the parts that bothers me the most, because I can sometimes see that the worry isn’t really helping, but I still can’t just… shut it off.

Like at work, I’ll tell myself, “Okay, I already checked the spreadsheet,” or “I already sent that email, it’s done,” but then my brain keeps circling back to it anyway. I’ll start wondering, what if I missed something, what if I sent it to the wrong person, what if there was an attachment issue, what if someone reads it the wrong way. And then it’s like one “what if” turns into five more before I can pull myself back.

Even when there isn’t one clear thing to worry about, my mind kind of searches for something. I can know logically that I did the task, or checked it, or handled it, but the thoughts keep looping like they don’t believe me. It’s exhausting because I’ll try to reassure myself, but it only works for a little bit, if at all.

I’m not really sure I’d describe it that way. I mean, I definitely get anxious and tense, and sometimes it can feel really uncomfortable, but I don’t know that I’ve had those sudden, out-of-nowhere waves where it’s like everything spikes all at once and I feel like something terrible is happening right that second.

I wish it were more like it came and went in a clear way, but it feels pretty constant lately.\n\nI mean, it may get louder or quieter depending on what I’m doing, but the baseline is usually there — like I’m wound up, or my body is bracing for the next bad thing. Even if I’m just sitting at my desk, not in the middle of anything dramatic, I’ll notice I’m tight and tense, like I’m already preparing for something to go wrong.\n\nAnd then even during things I actually want to enjoy, like dance night, it doesn’t fully switch off. I might be trying to have fun, but there’s still this tension underneath it, like I can’t completely relax. That’s actually one of the parts that bothers me the most, because I can sometimes see that the worry isn’t really helping, but I still can’t just… shut it off.\n\nLike at work, I’ll tell myself, “Okay, I already checked the spreadsheet,” or “I already sent that email, it’s done,” but then my brain keeps circling back to it anyway. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, it has. I think that’s actually where it started feeling less like “I’m just stressed” and more like… okay, this is getting in the way of my life.

At work, I’m a computer support specialist, so there are always tickets coming in, and I used to be pretty good at moving through them. Not perfectly, but I could prioritize and get things done. Lately I’ve been falling behind because I’ll get stuck double-checking things that should be simple. Like, I’ll review a response before sending it, then review it again, then worry I misunderstood the issue, or that I didn’t document it clearly enough, or that I might have missed some obvious step. And then meanwhile the queue keeps building.

It’s embarrassing, honestly, because coworkers have had to pick up some of the slack. No one has been cruel about it, but I can tell, and that makes me feel worse. I’ll see someone else take a ticket I should’ve handled, or they’ll gently ask if I’m okay, and then I start worrying about that too — like whether they think I’m unreliable.

And outside work, it’s affected things I actually care about. I’ve skipped several dance nights because getting myself there just feels like too much when I’m already overwhelmed. And I’ve missed mentoring sessions too, which I feel really guilty about, because that’s something I usually value and want to show up for. It’s not that I don’t care; it’s more like by the time it comes around, I feel so wound up and overloaded that I just can’t make myself go.

And once it does, it feels hard to let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you often experience physical symptoms like restlessness, fatigue, or muscle tension when you’re feeling anxious?\n\nPatient: Yes, especially the tension and that restless, wound-up feeling. It’s kind of the main reason I came in, honestly. I feel like my body is bracing for something bad to happen, even when there isn’t anything happening.\n\nLike I’ll just be sitting at my desk at work, answering tickets or staring at a screen, and my shoulders are already up near my ears, or I realize I’m holding myself really rigid. And it’s not even always because something stressful just happened. It’s more like my body is already preparing for the next thing to go wrong.\n\nEven when I’m supposed to be relaxing, like at dance night, I’ll notice I’m still tense inside. I want to enjoy it and just be there, but part of me feels on alert, like I can’t fully let go. It’s exhausting in a way, because I don’t feel like I get many moments where my body actually believes I’m safe or settled.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel this anxious or tense even when you’re completely alone and there’s no immediate situation, person, or task causing stress?\n\nPatient: Yes. Honestly, that’s part of what feels so frustrating about it, because it doesn’t always need a trigger. I can be completely by myself, with nothing actively happening, and I’ll still feel like my body is waiting for something bad to happen.\n\nLike I’ll just be sitting at my desk, or at home, or even trying to do something that should be enjoyable, and there’s this wound-up feeling in me. My shoulders get tight, and I feel like I’m bracing, almost like I’m preparing for the next problem before I even know what it is.\n\nAnd with dance night, too — even though I want to relax and enjoy it, I’ll notice I’m still tense inside. It’s not necessarily that someone said anything or that there’s some immediate task I’m stressed about. It’s more like my body hasn’t gotten the message that everything is okay. It just stays on alert.\n\nDoctor: How often do these anxious feelings and the physical tension happen—like, are they present most days, or do they come and go more sporadically?\n\nPatient: It’s most days. I wish it were more like it came and went in a clear way, but it feels pretty constant lately.\n\nI mean, it may get louder or quieter depending on what I’m doing, but the baseline is usually there — like I’m wound up, or my body is bracing for the next bad thing. Even if I’m just sitting at my desk, not in the middle of anything dramatic, I’ll notice I’m tight and tense, like I’m already preparing for something to go wrong.\n\nAnd then even during things I actually want to enjoy, like dance night, it doesn’t fully switch off. I might be trying to have fun, but there’s still this tension underneath it, like I can’t completely relax. So… yeah, I’d say it’s there most days, not just once in a while.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control or stop the worrying, even when you know it might not make sense or there's nothing specific to worry about?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually one of the parts that bothers me the most, because I can sometimes see that the worry isn’t really helping, but I still can’t just… shut it off.\n\nLike at work, I’ll tell myself, “Okay, I already checked the spreadsheet,” or “I already sent that email, it’s done,” but then my brain keeps circling back to it anyway. I’ll start wondering, what if I missed something, what if I sent it to the wrong person, what if there was an attachment issue, what if someone reads it the wrong way. And then it’s like one “what if” turns into five more before I can pull myself back.\n\nEven when there isn’t one clear thing to worry about, my mind kind of searches for something. I can know logically that I did the task, or checked it, or handled it, but the thoughts keep looping like they don’t believe me. It’s exhausting because I’ll try to reassure myself, but it only works for a little bit, if at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced sudden episodes where you feel an intense wave of fear or discomfort that comes on very quickly, like your heart starts racing, you feel short of breath, or like something terrible is going to happen right then and there?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d describe it that way. I mean, I definitely get anxious and tense, and sometimes it can feel really uncomfortable, but I don’t know that I’ve had those sudden, out-of-nowhere waves where it’s like everything spikes all at once and I feel like something terrible is happening right that second.\n\nFor me it feels more like this ongoing buildup or background tension, if that makes sense. Like I can be worrying and feeling keyed up for a long time, and it might get worse depending on what I’m thinking about, but it doesn’t usually feel like a distinct episode that suddenly comes on super fast and then passes. At least, I haven’t really noticed it in that clear way.\n\nI guess I do sometimes feel very uncomfortable in my body when I’m anxious, but I’m not sure about the heart racing or shortness of breath part specifically. If it happens, I don’t think I’ve been labeling it as a separate episode. It’s more like… I’m just anxious and wound up, not like a sudden attack of fear.\n\nDoctor: Has this constant worry and tension made it harder for you to function in your daily life, like at work, in social situations, or with personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. I think that’s actually where it started feeling less like “I’m just stressed” and more like… okay, this is getting in the way of my life.\n\nAt work, I’m a computer support specialist, so there are always tickets coming in, and I used to be pretty good at moving through them. Not perfectly, but I could prioritize and get things done. Lately I’ve been falling behind because I’ll get stuck double-checking things that should be simple. Like, I’ll review a response before sending it, then review it again, then worry I misunderstood the issue, or that I didn’t document it clearly enough, or that I might have missed some obvious step. And then meanwhile the queue keeps building.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly, because coworkers have had to pick up some of the slack. No one has been cruel about it, but I can tell, and that makes me feel worse. I’ll see someone else take a ticket I should’ve handled, or they’ll gently ask if I’m okay, and then I start worrying about that too — like whether they think I’m unreliable.\n\nAnd outside work, it’s affected things I actually care about. I’ve skipped several dance nights because getting myself there just feels like too much when I’m already overwhelmed. And I’ve missed mentoring sessions too, which I feel really guilty about, because that’s something I usually value and want to show up for. It’s not that I don’t care; it’s more like by the time it comes around, I feel so wound up and overloaded that I just can’t make myself go.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{
  "candidates": ["F41.1", "F43.11", "F40.00", "F40.10", "F32.0"],
  "note": "",
  "is_final": false
}

It’s not that I don’t care; it’s more like by the time it comes around, I feel so wound up and overloaded that I just can’t make myself go.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you have trouble sleeping because your mind keeps racing or you’re unable to relax, even when you’re tired?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 29 / computer_support_specialist / graduate\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Sleep_Disturbance\nmanifestation: \"Most nights I get into bed exhausted, but my brain keeps replaying loan notices, work tickets, and things I might have missed until it's 2 or 3 in the morning, and even when I sleep I wake up feeling like I barely rested.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, definitely. That’s been happening most nights, honestly. I’ll be tired — like, really tired — and I’ll get into bed thinking I should fall asleep quickly because my body feels worn out, but then as soon as it’s quiet my brain just starts going.

It’ll replay work tickets, like whether I handled something correctly or missed a step, or whether I documented something clearly enough. Then it jumps to loan notices or bills or something I saw in the mail, and I start thinking, did I forget a deadline, did I misunderstand something, what if there’s a problem I didn’t catch. And then somehow I’m mentally going through all these little details that I can’t do anything about at that moment anyway.

I’ll try to tell myself, “You’re in bed, you can deal with it tomorrow,” but it doesn’t really settle. I keep feeling like I need to solve it before I can relax, except there’s nothing I can actually solve at 1 or 2 in the morning. A lot of nights I look at the clock and it’s suddenly 2 or 3 a.m., and then I get stressed about how little sleep I’m going to get, which makes it even harder.

And even when I do finally sleep, it doesn’t feel very restful. I wake up feeling like I barely slept, like my mind was still half-running the whole time.

Like my body is constantly bracing for something bad to happen, even when there’s no obvious reason. I can be sitting at my desk at work, doing computer support stuff, and I’m technically just answering tickets or trying to focus, but my shoulders are tight and I feel like I’m on alert.\n\nAnd it’s not only at work. Even when I’m doing something I normally enjoy, like dance night, I still feel this tension in me, like I can’t fully relax into it. It’s like part of me is waiting for the next problem, the next bad thing, and I’m tired of feeling like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about different things in your life, like work, relationships, or other everyday issues, even when there isn’t a clear reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. It’s like my worry doesn’t stay in one place anymore. At first I think I was more focused on the condo and practical stuff around that, but now it kind of jumps around all day.\n\nLike I’ll be wondering if my cousin is upset with me because of something I said, and I’ll replay the conversation in my head and try to figure out if her tone seemed different. Then I’ll be at work and suddenly it’s about whether I’m going to make some dumb mistake on a support call, or miss something obvious, or sound incompetent. And then later it turns into bigger life stuff, like how I’m ever going to save enough money, or move forward, or feel like I’m not falling behind everyone else.\n\nIt’s not always that there’s a clear problem happening right then. Sometimes there’s nothing specific, and my mind just… finds something to attach to. And once it does, it feels hard to let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you often experience physical symptoms like restlessness, fatigue, or muscle tension when you’re feeling anxious?\n\nPatient: Yes, especially the tension and that restless, wound-up feeling. It’s kind of the main reason I came in, honestly. I feel like my body is bracing for something bad to happen, even when there isn’t anything happening.\n\nLike I’ll just be sitting at my desk at work, answering tickets or staring at a screen, and my shoulders are already up near my ears, or I realize I’m holding myself really rigid. And it’s not even always because something stressful just happened. It’s more like my body is already preparing for the next thing to go wrong.\n\nEven when I’m supposed to be relaxing, like at dance night, I’ll notice I’m still tense inside. I want to enjoy it and just be there, but part of me feels on alert, like I can’t fully let go. It’s exhausting in a way, because I don’t feel like I get many moments where my body actually believes I’m safe or settled.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel this anxious or tense even when you’re completely alone and there’s no immediate situation, person, or task causing stress?\n\nPatient: Yes. Honestly, that’s part of what feels so frustrating about it, because it doesn’t always need a trigger. I can be completely by myself, with nothing actively happening, and I’ll still feel like my body is waiting for something bad to happen.\n\nLike I’ll just be sitting at my desk, or at home, or even trying to do something that should be enjoyable, and there’s this wound-up feeling in me. My shoulders get tight, and I feel like I’m bracing, almost like I’m preparing for the next problem before I even know what it is.\n\nAnd with dance night, too — even though I want to relax and enjoy it, I’ll notice I’m still tense inside. It’s not necessarily that someone said anything or that there’s some immediate task I’m stressed about. It’s more like my body hasn’t gotten the message that everything is okay. It just stays on alert.\n\nDoctor: How often do these anxious feelings and the physical tension happen—like, are they present most days, or do they come and go more sporadically?\n\nPatient: It’s most days. I wish it were more like it came and went in a clear way, but it feels pretty constant lately.\n\nI mean, it may get louder or quieter depending on what I’m doing, but the baseline is usually there — like I’m wound up, or my body is bracing for the next bad thing. Even if I’m just sitting at my desk, not in the middle of anything dramatic, I’ll notice I’m tight and tense, like I’m already preparing for something to go wrong.\n\nAnd then even during things I actually want to enjoy, like dance night, it doesn’t fully switch off. I might be trying to have fun, but there’s still this tension underneath it, like I can’t completely relax. So… yeah, I’d say it’s there most days, not just once in a while.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control or stop the worrying, even when you know it might not make sense or there's nothing specific to worry about?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually one of the parts that bothers me the most, because I can sometimes see that the worry isn’t really helping, but I still can’t just… shut it off.\n\nLike at work, I’ll tell myself, “Okay, I already checked the spreadsheet,” or “I already sent that email, it’s done,” but then my brain keeps circling back to it anyway. I’ll start wondering, what if I missed something, what if I sent it to the wrong person, what if there was an attachment issue, what if someone reads it the wrong way. And then it’s like one “what if” turns into five more before I can pull myself back.\n\nEven when there isn’t one clear thing to worry about, my mind kind of searches for something. I can know logically that I did the task, or checked it, or handled it, but the thoughts keep looping like they don’t believe me. It’s exhausting because I’ll try to reassure myself, but it only works for a little bit, if at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced sudden episodes where you feel an intense wave of fear or discomfort that comes on very quickly, like your heart starts racing, you feel short of breath, or like something terrible is going to happen right then and there?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d describe it that way. I mean, I definitely get anxious and tense, and sometimes it can feel really uncomfortable, but I don’t know that I’ve had those sudden, out-of-nowhere waves where it’s like everything spikes all at once and I feel like something terrible is happening right that second.\n\nFor me it feels more like this ongoing buildup or background tension, if that makes sense. Like I can be worrying and feeling keyed up for a long time, and it might get worse depending on what I’m thinking about, but it doesn’t usually feel like a distinct episode that suddenly comes on super fast and then passes. At least, I haven’t really noticed it in that clear way.\n\nI guess I do sometimes feel very uncomfortable in my body when I’m anxious, but I’m not sure about the heart racing or shortness of breath part specifically. If it happens, I don’t think I’ve been labeling it as a separate episode. It’s more like… I’m just anxious and wound up, not like a sudden attack of fear.\n\nDoctor: Has this constant worry and tension made it harder for you to function in your daily life, like at work, in social situations, or with personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. I think that’s actually where it started feeling less like “I’m just stressed” and more like… okay, this is getting in the way of my life.\n\nAt work, I’m a computer support specialist, so there are always tickets coming in, and I used to be pretty good at moving through them. Not perfectly, but I could prioritize and get things done. Lately I’ve been falling behind because I’ll get stuck double-checking things that should be simple. Like, I’ll review a response before sending it, then review it again, then worry I misunderstood the issue, or that I didn’t document it clearly enough, or that I might have missed some obvious step. And then meanwhile the queue keeps building.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly, because coworkers have had to pick up some of the slack. No one has been cruel about it, but I can tell, and that makes me feel worse. I’ll see someone else take a ticket I should’ve handled, or they’ll gently ask if I’m okay, and then I start worrying about that too — like whether they think I’m unreliable.\n\nAnd outside work, it’s affected things I actually care about. I’ve skipped several dance nights because getting myself there just feels like too much when I’m already overwhelmed. And I’ve missed mentoring sessions too, which I feel really guilty about, because that’s something I usually value and want to show up for. It’s not that I don’t care; it’s more like by the time it comes around, I feel so wound up and overloaded that I just can’t make myself go.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble sleeping because your mind keeps racing or you’re unable to relax, even when you’re tired?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I mean, I definitely get anxious and tense, and sometimes it can feel really uncomfortable, but I don’t know that I’ve had those sudden, out-of-nowhere waves where it’s like everything spikes all at once and I feel like something terrible is happening right that second.\n\nFor me it feels more like this ongoing buildup or background tension, if that makes sense. Like I can be worrying and feeling keyed up for a long time, and it might get worse depending on what I’m thinking about, but it doesn’t usually feel like a distinct episode that suddenly comes on super fast and then passes. At least, I haven’t really noticed it in that clear way.\n\nI guess I do sometimes feel very uncomfortable in my body when I’m anxious, but I’m not sure about the heart racing or shortness of breath part specifically. If it happens, I don’t think I’ve been labeling it as a separate episode. It’s more like… I’m just anxious and wound up, not like a sudden attack of fear.\n\nDoctor: Has this constant worry and tension made it harder for you to function in your daily life, like at work, in social situations, or with personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. I think that’s actually where it started feeling less like “I’m just stressed” and more like… okay, this is getting in the way of my life.\n\nAt work, I’m a computer support specialist, so there are always tickets coming in, and I used to be pretty good at moving through them. Not perfectly, but I could prioritize and get things done. Lately I’ve been falling behind because I’ll get stuck double-checking things that should be simple. Like, I’ll review a response before sending it, then review it again, then worry I misunderstood the issue, or that I didn’t document it clearly enough, or that I might have missed some obvious step. And then meanwhile the queue keeps building.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly, because coworkers have had to pick up some of the slack. No one has been cruel about it, but I can tell, and that makes me feel worse. I’ll see someone else take a ticket I should’ve handled, or they’ll gently ask if I’m okay, and then I start worrying about that too — like whether they think I’m unreliable.\n\nAnd outside work, it’s affected things I actually care about. I’ve skipped several dance nights because getting myself there just feels like too much when I’m already overwhelmed. And I’ve missed mentoring sessions too, which I feel really guilty about, because that’s something I usually value and want to show up for. It’s not that I don’t care; it’s more like by the time it comes around, I feel so wound up and overloaded that I just can’t make myself go.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble sleeping because your mind keeps racing or you’re unable to relax, even when you’re tired?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. That’s been happening most nights, honestly. I’ll be tired — like, really tired — and I’ll get into bed thinking I should fall asleep quickly because my body feels worn out, but then as soon as it’s quiet my brain just starts going.\n\nIt’ll replay work tickets, like whether I handled something correctly or missed a step, or whether I documented something clearly enough. Then it jumps to loan notices or bills or something I saw in the mail, and I start thinking, did I forget a deadline, did I misunderstand something, what if there’s a problem I didn’t catch. And then somehow I’m mentally going through all these little details that I can’t do anything about at that moment anyway.\n\nI’ll try to tell myself, “You’re in bed, you can deal with it tomorrow,” but it doesn’t really settle. I keep feeling like I need to solve it before I can relax, except there’s nothing I can actually solve at 1 or 2 in the morning. A lot of nights I look at the clock and it’s suddenly 2 or 3 a.m., and then I get stressed about how little sleep I’m going to get, which makes it even harder.\n\nAnd even when I do finally sleep, it doesn’t feel very restful. I wake up feeling like I barely slept, like my mind was still half-running the whole time.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed yourself feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even outside of the times when you’re feeling anxious or wound up?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "patient_turn_index: 10\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."
  }
]

{"key":"None","name":"None","description":"None","reason":"The question is asking about depressive symptoms such as low mood, hopelessness, or loss of interest, which are not covered by the provided symptom profiles."}

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure, honestly. I wouldn’t say I feel hopeless in a clear way, like I’ve given up on everything or anything like that. It’s more hard for me to separate what’s “feeling down” from just being exhausted and overwhelmed all the time.

Like, with things I usually enjoy, sometimes I don’t go or I don’t feel up for them, but I think that’s more because I’m tense or drained rather than because I don’t care about them anymore. If I actually imagine doing them on a day where I felt calmer, I think I would still want to. So I don’t know that it’s a loss of interest exactly.

I do get discouraged with myself, especially when I feel like I’m not keeping up or I’m disappointing people, but it feels tied to being wound up and stressed rather than this separate low mood that’s there on its own. I haven’t really noticed a separate “down” feeling outside of that, at least not in a way I could clearly point to.

Like my body is constantly bracing for something bad to happen, even when there’s no obvious reason. I can be sitting at my desk at work, doing computer support stuff, and I’m technically just answering tickets or trying to focus, but my shoulders are tight and I feel like I’m on alert.\n\nAnd it’s not only at work. Even when I’m doing something I normally enjoy, like dance night, I still feel this tension in me, like I can’t fully relax into it. It’s like part of me is waiting for the next problem, the next bad thing, and I’m tired of feeling like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about different things in your life, like work, relationships, or other everyday issues, even when there isn’t a clear reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. It’s like my worry doesn’t stay in one place anymore. At first I think I was more focused on the condo and practical stuff around that, but now it kind of jumps around all day.\n\nLike I’ll be wondering if my cousin is upset with me because of something I said, and I’ll replay the conversation in my head and try to figure out if her tone seemed different. Then I’ll be at work and suddenly it’s about whether I’m going to make some dumb mistake on a support call, or miss something obvious, or sound incompetent. And then later it turns into bigger life stuff, like how I’m ever going to save enough money, or move forward, or feel like I’m not falling behind everyone else.\n\nIt’s not always that there’s a clear problem happening right then. Sometimes there’s nothing specific, and my mind just… finds something to attach to. And once it does, it feels hard to let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you often experience physical symptoms like restlessness, fatigue, or muscle tension when you’re feeling anxious?\n\nPatient: Yes, especially the tension and that restless, wound-up feeling. It’s kind of the main reason I came in, honestly. I feel like my body is bracing for something bad to happen, even when there isn’t anything happening.\n\nLike I’ll just be sitting at my desk at work, answering tickets or staring at a screen, and my shoulders are already up near my ears, or I realize I’m holding myself really rigid. And it’s not even always because something stressful just happened. It’s more like my body is already preparing for the next thing to go wrong.\n\nEven when I’m supposed to be relaxing, like at dance night, I’ll notice I’m still tense inside. I want to enjoy it and just be there, but part of me feels on alert, like I can’t fully let go. It’s exhausting in a way, because I don’t feel like I get many moments where my body actually believes I’m safe or settled.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel this anxious or tense even when you’re completely alone and there’s no immediate situation, person, or task causing stress?\n\nPatient: Yes. Honestly, that’s part of what feels so frustrating about it, because it doesn’t always need a trigger. I can be completely by myself, with nothing actively happening, and I’ll still feel like my body is waiting for something bad to happen.\n\nLike I’ll just be sitting at my desk, or at home, or even trying to do something that should be enjoyable, and there’s this wound-up feeling in me. My shoulders get tight, and I feel like I’m bracing, almost like I’m preparing for the next problem before I even know what it is.\n\nAnd with dance night, too — even though I want to relax and enjoy it, I’ll notice I’m still tense inside. It’s not necessarily that someone said anything or that there’s some immediate task I’m stressed about. It’s more like my body hasn’t gotten the message that everything is okay. It just stays on alert.\n\nDoctor: How often do these anxious feelings and the physical tension happen—like, are they present most days, or do they come and go more sporadically?\n\nPatient: It’s most days. I wish it were more like it came and went in a clear way, but it feels pretty constant lately.\n\nI mean, it may get louder or quieter depending on what I’m doing, but the baseline is usually there — like I’m wound up, or my body is bracing for the next bad thing. Even if I’m just sitting at my desk, not in the middle of anything dramatic, I’ll notice I’m tight and tense, like I’m already preparing for something to go wrong.\n\nAnd then even during things I actually want to enjoy, like dance night, it doesn’t fully switch off. I might be trying to have fun, but there’s still this tension underneath it, like I can’t completely relax. So… yeah, I’d say it’s there most days, not just once in a while.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control or stop the worrying, even when you know it might not make sense or there's nothing specific to worry about?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually one of the parts that bothers me the most, because I can sometimes see that the worry isn’t really helping, but I still can’t just… shut it off.\n\nLike at work, I’ll tell myself, “Okay, I already checked the spreadsheet,” or “I already sent that email, it’s done,” but then my brain keeps circling back to it anyway. I’ll start wondering, what if I missed something, what if I sent it to the wrong person, what if there was an attachment issue, what if someone reads it the wrong way. And then it’s like one “what if” turns into five more before I can pull myself back.\n\nEven when there isn’t one clear thing to worry about, my mind kind of searches for something. I can know logically that I did the task, or checked it, or handled it, but the thoughts keep looping like they don’t believe me. It’s exhausting because I’ll try to reassure myself, but it only works for a little bit, if at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced sudden episodes where you feel an intense wave of fear or discomfort that comes on very quickly, like your heart starts racing, you feel short of breath, or like something terrible is going to happen right then and there?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d describe it that way. My shoulders get tight, and I feel like I’m bracing, almost like I’m preparing for the next problem before I even know what it is.\n\nAnd with dance night, too — even though I want to relax and enjoy it, I’ll notice I’m still tense inside. It’s not necessarily that someone said anything or that there’s some immediate task I’m stressed about. It’s more like my body hasn’t gotten the message that everything is okay. It just stays on alert.\n\nDoctor: How often do these anxious feelings and the physical tension happen—like, are they present most days, or do they come and go more sporadically?\n\nPatient: It’s most days. I wish it were more like it came and went in a clear way, but it feels pretty constant lately.\n\nI mean, it may get louder or quieter depending on what I’m doing, but the baseline is usually there — like I’m wound up, or my body is bracing for the next bad thing. Even if I’m just sitting at my desk, not in the middle of anything dramatic, I’ll notice I’m tight and tense, like I’m already preparing for something to go wrong.\n\nAnd then even during things I actually want to enjoy, like dance night, it doesn’t fully switch off. I might be trying to have fun, but there’s still this tension underneath it, like I can’t completely relax. So… yeah, I’d say it’s there most days, not just once in a while.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control or stop the worrying, even when you know it might not make sense or there's nothing specific to worry about?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually one of the parts that bothers me the most, because I can sometimes see that the worry isn’t really helping, but I still can’t just… shut it off.\n\nLike at work, I’ll tell myself, “Okay, I already checked the spreadsheet,” or “I already sent that email, it’s done,” but then my brain keeps circling back to it anyway. I’ll start wondering, what if I missed something, what if I sent it to the wrong person, what if there was an attachment issue, what if someone reads it the wrong way. And then it’s like one “what if” turns into five more before I can pull myself back.\n\nEven when there isn’t one clear thing to worry about, my mind kind of searches for something. I can know logically that I did the task, or checked it, or handled it, but the thoughts keep looping like they don’t believe me. It’s exhausting because I’ll try to reassure myself, but it only works for a little bit, if at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced sudden episodes where you feel an intense wave of fear or discomfort that comes on very quickly, like your heart starts racing, you feel short of breath, or like something terrible is going to happen right then and there?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d describe it that way. I mean, I definitely get anxious and tense, and sometimes it can feel really uncomfortable, but I don’t know that I’ve had those sudden, out-of-nowhere waves where it’s like everything spikes all at once and I feel like something terrible is happening right that second.\n\nFor me it feels more like this ongoing buildup or background tension, if that makes sense. Like I can be worrying and feeling keyed up for a long time, and it might get worse depending on what I’m thinking about, but it doesn’t usually feel like a distinct episode that suddenly comes on super fast and then passes. At least, I haven’t really noticed it in that clear way.\n\nI guess I do sometimes feel very uncomfortable in my body when I’m anxious, but I’m not sure about the heart racing or shortness of breath part specifically. If it happens, I don’t think I’ve been labeling it as a separate episode. It’s more like… I’m just anxious and wound up, not like a sudden attack of fear.\n\nDoctor: Has this constant worry and tension made it harder for you to function in your daily life, like at work, in social situations, or with personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. I think that’s actually where it started feeling less like “I’m just stressed” and more like… okay, this is getting in the way of my life.\n\nAt work, I’m a computer support specialist, so there are always tickets coming in, and I used to be pretty good at moving through them. Not perfectly, but I could prioritize and get things done. Lately I’ve been falling behind because I’ll get stuck double-checking things that should be simple. Like, I’ll review a response before sending it, then review it again, then worry I misunderstood the issue, or that I didn’t document it clearly enough, or that I might have missed some obvious step. And then meanwhile the queue keeps building.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly, because coworkers have had to pick up some of the slack. No one has been cruel about it, but I can tell, and that makes me feel worse. I’ll see someone else take a ticket I should’ve handled, or they’ll gently ask if I’m okay, and then I start worrying about that too — like whether they think I’m unreliable.\n\nAnd outside work, it’s affected things I actually care about. I’ve skipped several dance nights because getting myself there just feels like too much when I’m already overwhelmed. And I’ve missed mentoring sessions too, which I feel really guilty about, because that’s something I usually value and want to show up for. It’s not that I don’t care; it’s more like by the time it comes around, I feel so wound up and overloaded that I just can’t make myself go.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble sleeping because your mind keeps racing or you’re unable to relax, even when you’re tired?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. That’s been happening most nights, honestly. I’ll be tired — like, really tired — and I’ll get into bed thinking I should fall asleep quickly because my body feels worn out, but then as soon as it’s quiet my brain just starts going.\n\nIt’ll replay work tickets, like whether I handled something correctly or missed a step, or whether I documented something clearly enough. Then it jumps to loan notices or bills or something I saw in the mail, and I start thinking, did I forget a deadline, did I misunderstand something, what if there’s a problem I didn’t catch. And then somehow I’m mentally going through all these little details that I can’t do anything about at that moment anyway.\n\nI’ll try to tell myself, “You’re in bed, you can deal with it tomorrow,” but it doesn’t really settle. I keep feeling like I need to solve it before I can relax, except there’s nothing I can actually solve at 1 or 2 in the morning. A lot of nights I look at the clock and it’s suddenly 2 or 3 a.m., and then I get stressed about how little sleep I’m going to get, which makes it even harder.\n\nAnd even when I do finally sleep, it doesn’t feel very restful. I wake up feeling like I barely slept, like my mind was still half-running the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even outside of the times when you’re feeling anxious or wound up?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I wouldn’t say I feel hopeless in a clear way, like I’ve given up on everything or anything like that. It’s more hard for me to separate what’s “feeling down” from just being exhausted and overwhelmed all the time.\n\nLike, with things I usually enjoy, sometimes I don’t go or I don’t feel up for them, but I think that’s more because I’m tense or drained rather than because I don’t care about them anymore. If I actually imagine doing them on a day where I felt calmer, I think I would still want to. So I don’t know that it’s a loss of interest exactly.\n\nI do get discouraged with myself, especially when I feel like I’m not keeping up or I’m disappointing people, but it feels tied to being wound up and stressed rather than this separate low mood that’s there on its own. I haven’t really noticed a separate “down” feeling outside of that, at least not in a way I could clearly point to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Yes. It’s not just one big thing anymore, which is part of why it feels so exhausting. At first I could kind of tell myself, okay, I’m worried about the condo situation, that’s stressful, that makes sense. But now it’s like my mind doesn’t stay there — it jumps around to anything it can grab onto.

Then five minutes later I’m worrying about work, like whether I’m going to make some mistake on a support call or misunderstand what someone is asking for. And then it’ll shift again to money, like how I’m supposed to save enough or move forward in life if I’m already feeling behind.

It can be really small things too, not just major problems. I’ll take something ordinary and then my brain turns it into this whole chain of “what ifs.” It feels like I’m constantly scanning for what might go wrong next, even when there isn’t one clear emergency in front of me.

I might be trying to have fun, but there’s still this tension underneath it, like I can’t completely relax. So… yeah, I’d say it’s there most days, not just once in a while.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control or stop the worrying, even when you know it might not make sense or there's nothing specific to worry about?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually one of the parts that bothers me the most, because I can sometimes see that the worry isn’t really helping, but I still can’t just… shut it off.\n\nLike at work, I’ll tell myself, “Okay, I already checked the spreadsheet,” or “I already sent that email, it’s done,” but then my brain keeps circling back to it anyway. I’ll start wondering, what if I missed something, what if I sent it to the wrong person, what if there was an attachment issue, what if someone reads it the wrong way. And then it’s like one “what if” turns into five more before I can pull myself back.\n\nEven when there isn’t one clear thing to worry about, my mind kind of searches for something. I can know logically that I did the task, or checked it, or handled it, but the thoughts keep looping like they don’t believe me. It’s exhausting because I’ll try to reassure myself, but it only works for a little bit, if at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced sudden episodes where you feel an intense wave of fear or discomfort that comes on very quickly, like your heart starts racing, you feel short of breath, or like something terrible is going to happen right then and there?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d describe it that way. I mean, I definitely get anxious and tense, and sometimes it can feel really uncomfortable, but I don’t know that I’ve had those sudden, out-of-nowhere waves where it’s like everything spikes all at once and I feel like something terrible is happening right that second.\n\nFor me it feels more like this ongoing buildup or background tension, if that makes sense. Like I can be worrying and feeling keyed up for a long time, and it might get worse depending on what I’m thinking about, but it doesn’t usually feel like a distinct episode that suddenly comes on super fast and then passes. At least, I haven’t really noticed it in that clear way.\n\nI guess I do sometimes feel very uncomfortable in my body when I’m anxious, but I’m not sure about the heart racing or shortness of breath part specifically. If it happens, I don’t think I’ve been labeling it as a separate episode. It’s more like… I’m just anxious and wound up, not like a sudden attack of fear.\n\nDoctor: Has this constant worry and tension made it harder for you to function in your daily life, like at work, in social situations, or with personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. I think that’s actually where it started feeling less like “I’m just stressed” and more like… okay, this is getting in the way of my life.\n\nAt work, I’m a computer support specialist, so there are always tickets coming in, and I used to be pretty good at moving through them. Not perfectly, but I could prioritize and get things done. Lately I’ve been falling behind because I’ll get stuck double-checking things that should be simple. Like, I’ll review a response before sending it, then review it again, then worry I misunderstood the issue, or that I didn’t document it clearly enough, or that I might have missed some obvious step. And then meanwhile the queue keeps building.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly, because coworkers have had to pick up some of the slack. No one has been cruel about it, but I can tell, and that makes me feel worse. I’ll see someone else take a ticket I should’ve handled, or they’ll gently ask if I’m okay, and then I start worrying about that too — like whether they think I’m unreliable.\n\nAnd outside work, it’s affected things I actually care about. I’ve skipped several dance nights because getting myself there just feels like too much when I’m already overwhelmed. And I’ve missed mentoring sessions too, which I feel really guilty about, because that’s something I usually value and want to show up for. It’s not that I don’t care; it’s more like by the time it comes around, I feel so wound up and overloaded that I just can’t make myself go.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble sleeping because your mind keeps racing or you’re unable to relax, even when you’re tired?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. That’s been happening most nights, honestly. I’ll be tired — like, really tired — and I’ll get into bed thinking I should fall asleep quickly because my body feels worn out, but then as soon as it’s quiet my brain just starts going.\n\nIt’ll replay work tickets, like whether I handled something correctly or missed a step, or whether I documented something clearly enough. Then it jumps to loan notices or bills or something I saw in the mail, and I start thinking, did I forget a deadline, did I misunderstand something, what if there’s a problem I didn’t catch. And then somehow I’m mentally going through all these little details that I can’t do anything about at that moment anyway.\n\nI’ll try to tell myself, “You’re in bed, you can deal with it tomorrow,” but it doesn’t really settle. I keep feeling like I need to solve it before I can relax, except there’s nothing I can actually solve at 1 or 2 in the morning. A lot of nights I look at the clock and it’s suddenly 2 or 3 a.m., and then I get stressed about how little sleep I’m going to get, which makes it even harder.\n\nAnd even when I do finally sleep, it doesn’t feel very restful. I wake up feeling like I barely slept, like my mind was still half-running the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even outside of the times when you’re feeling anxious or wound up?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I wouldn’t say I feel hopeless in a clear way, like I’ve given up on everything or anything like that. It’s more hard for me to separate what’s “feeling down” from just being exhausted and overwhelmed all the time.\n\nLike, with things I usually enjoy, sometimes I don’t go or I don’t feel up for them, but I think that’s more because I’m tense or drained rather than because I don’t care about them anymore. If I actually imagine doing them on a day where I felt calmer, I think I would still want to. So I don’t know that it’s a loss of interest exactly.\n\nI do get discouraged with myself, especially when I feel like I’m not keeping up or I’m disappointing people, but it feels tied to being wound up and stressed rather than this separate low mood that’s there on its own. I haven’t really noticed a separate “down” feeling outside of that, at least not in a way I could clearly point to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a variety of things, even small ones, not just major life events or problems?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s not just one big thing anymore, which is part of why it feels so exhausting. At first I could kind of tell myself, okay, I’m worried about the condo situation, that’s stressful, that makes sense. But now it’s like my mind doesn’t stay there — it jumps around to anything it can grab onto.\n\nI’ll worry that my cousin is upset with me because of something small, like if a text sounded a little short or if I didn’t respond the “right” way. Then five minutes later I’m worrying about work, like whether I’m going to make some mistake on a support call or misunderstand what someone is asking for. And then it’ll shift again to money, like how I’m supposed to save enough or move forward in life if I’m already feeling behind.\n\nIt can be really small things too, not just major problems. I’ll take something ordinary and then my brain turns it into this whole chain of “what ifs.” It feels like I’m constantly scanning for what might go wrong next, even when there isn’t one clear emergency in front of me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I’ll be tired — like, really tired — and I’ll get into bed thinking I should fall asleep quickly because my body feels worn out, but then as soon as it’s quiet my brain just starts going.\n\nIt’ll replay work tickets, like whether I handled something correctly or missed a step, or whether I documented something clearly enough. Then it jumps to loan notices or bills or something I saw in the mail, and I start thinking, did I forget a deadline, did I misunderstand something, what if there’s a problem I didn’t catch. And then somehow I’m mentally going through all these little details that I can’t do anything about at that moment anyway.\n\nI’ll try to tell myself, “You’re in bed, you can deal with it tomorrow,” but it doesn’t really settle. I keep feeling like I need to solve it before I can relax, except there’s nothing I can actually solve at 1 or 2 in the morning. A lot of nights I look at the clock and it’s suddenly 2 or 3 a.m., and then I get stressed about how little sleep I’m going to get, which makes it even harder.\n\nAnd even when I do finally sleep, it doesn’t feel very restful. I wake up feeling like I barely slept, like my mind was still half-running the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even outside of the times when you’re feeling anxious or wound up?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I wouldn’t say I feel hopeless in a clear way, like I’ve given up on everything or anything like that. It’s more hard for me to separate what’s “feeling down” from just being exhausted and overwhelmed all the time.\n\nLike, with things I usually enjoy, sometimes I don’t go or I don’t feel up for them, but I think that’s more because I’m tense or drained rather than because I don’t care about them anymore. If I actually imagine doing them on a day where I felt calmer, I think I would still want to. So I don’t know that it’s a loss of interest exactly.\n\nI do get discouraged with myself, especially when I feel like I’m not keeping up or I’m disappointing people, but it feels tied to being wound up and stressed rather than this separate low mood that’s there on its own. I haven’t really noticed a separate “down” feeling outside of that, at least not in a way I could clearly point to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a variety of things, even small ones, not just major life events or problems?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s not just one big thing anymore, which is part of why it feels so exhausting. At first I could kind of tell myself, okay, I’m worried about the condo situation, that’s stressful, that makes sense. But now it’s like my mind doesn’t stay there — it jumps around to anything it can grab onto.\n\nI’ll worry that my cousin is upset with me because of something small, like if a text sounded a little short or if I didn’t respond the “right” way. Then five minutes later I’m worrying about work, like whether I’m going to make some mistake on a support call or misunderstand what someone is asking for. And then it’ll shift again to money, like how I’m supposed to save enough or move forward in life if I’m already feeling behind.\n\nIt can be really small things too, not just major problems. I’ll take something ordinary and then my brain turns it into this whole chain of “what ifs.” It feels like I’m constantly scanning for what might go wrong next, even when there isn’t one clear emergency in front of me.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever experienced sudden, intense episodes of fear where you felt like something terrible was happening, such as your heart racing, feeling dizzy, or short of breath, even when there was no real danger?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 29 / computer_support_specialist / graduate\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so… not in that really sudden, out-of-nowhere way you’re describing. I mean, I definitely get anxious and worked up, and sometimes it can feel intense, but it’s usually more like it builds because I’m thinking about something or worrying through a situation.

I don’t really remember having an episode where, all of a sudden, I felt like I was in immediate danger for no clear reason, like “something terrible is happening right now.” I might feel tense or uneasy, but I don’t think I’ve had that kind of abrupt wave of fear with the heart racing or dizziness or not being able to breathe in the way people describe.

I guess if anything, my fear tends to be more drawn out and tied to my thoughts, not like a sudden attack that peaks quickly. At least, not that I’ve noticed.

Like my body is constantly bracing for something bad to happen, even when there’s no obvious reason. I can be sitting at my desk at work, doing computer support stuff, and I’m technically just answering tickets or trying to focus, but my shoulders are tight and I feel like I’m on alert.\n\nAnd it’s not only at work. Even when I’m doing something I normally enjoy, like dance night, I still feel this tension in me, like I can’t fully relax into it. It’s like part of me is waiting for the next problem, the next bad thing, and I’m tired of feeling like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about different things in your life, like work, relationships, or other everyday issues, even when there isn’t a clear reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. It’s like my worry doesn’t stay in one place anymore. At first I think I was more focused on the condo and practical stuff around that, but now it kind of jumps around all day.\n\nLike I’ll be wondering if my cousin is upset with me because of something I said, and I’ll replay the conversation in my head and try to figure out if her tone seemed different. Then I’ll be at work and suddenly it’s about whether I’m going to make some dumb mistake on a support call, or miss something obvious, or sound incompetent. And then later it turns into bigger life stuff, like how I’m ever going to save enough money, or move forward, or feel like I’m not falling behind everyone else.\n\nIt’s not always that there’s a clear problem happening right then. Sometimes there’s nothing specific, and my mind just… finds something to attach to. It’s kind of the main reason I came in, honestly. I feel like my body is bracing for something bad to happen, even when there isn’t anything happening.\n\nLike I’ll just be sitting at my desk at work, answering tickets or staring at a screen, and my shoulders are already up near my ears, or I realize I’m holding myself really rigid. And it’s not even always because something stressful just happened. It’s more like my body is already preparing for the next thing to go wrong.\n\nEven when I’m supposed to be relaxing, like at dance night, I’ll notice I’m still tense inside. I want to enjoy it and just be there, but part of me feels on alert, like I can’t fully let go. It’s exhausting in a way, because I don’t feel like I get many moments where my body actually believes I’m safe or settled.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel this anxious or tense even when you’re completely alone and there’s no immediate situation, person, or task causing stress?\n\nPatient: Yes. Honestly, that’s part of what feels so frustrating about it, because it doesn’t always need a trigger. I can be completely by myself, with nothing actively happening, and I’ll still feel like my body is waiting for something bad to happen.\n\nLike I’ll just be sitting at my desk, or at home, or even trying to do something that should be enjoyable, and there’s this wound-up feeling in me. My shoulders get tight, and I feel like I’m bracing, almost like I’m preparing for the next problem before I even know what it is.\n\nAnd with dance night, too — even though I want to relax and enjoy it, I’ll notice I’m still tense inside. It’s not necessarily that someone said anything or that there’s some immediate task I’m stressed about. It’s more like my body hasn’t gotten the message that everything is okay. It just stays on alert.\n\nDoctor: How often do these anxious feelings and the physical tension happen—like, are they present most days, or do they come and go more sporadically?\n\nPatient: It’s most days. I wish it were more like it came and went in a clear way, but it feels pretty constant lately.\n\nI mean, it may get louder or quieter depending on what I’m doing, but the baseline is usually there — like I’m wound up, or my body is bracing for the next bad thing. Even if I’m just sitting at my desk, not in the middle of anything dramatic, I’ll notice I’m tight and tense, like I’m already preparing for something to go wrong.\n\nAnd then even during things I actually want to enjoy, like dance night, it doesn’t fully switch off. I might be trying to have fun, but there’s still this tension underneath it, like I can’t completely relax. So… yeah, I’d say it’s there most days, not just once in a while.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control or stop the worrying, even when you know it might not make sense or there's nothing specific to worry about?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually one of the parts that bothers me the most, because I can sometimes see that the worry isn’t really helping, but I still can’t just… shut it off.\n\nLike at work, I’ll tell myself, “Okay, I already checked the spreadsheet,” or “I already sent that email, it’s done,” but then my brain keeps circling back to it anyway. I’ll start wondering, what if I missed something, what if I sent it to the wrong person, what if there was an attachment issue, what if someone reads it the wrong way. And then it’s like one “what if” turns into five more before I can pull myself back.\n\nEven when there isn’t one clear thing to worry about, my mind kind of searches for something. I can know logically that I did the task, or checked it, or handled it, but the thoughts keep looping like they don’t believe me. It’s exhausting because I’ll try to reassure myself, but it only works for a little bit, if at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced sudden episodes where you feel an intense wave of fear or discomfort that comes on very quickly, like your heart starts racing, you feel short of breath, or like something terrible is going to happen right then and there?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d describe it that way. I mean, I definitely get anxious and tense, and sometimes it can feel really uncomfortable, but I don’t know that I’ve had those sudden, out-of-nowhere waves where it’s like everything spikes all at once and I feel like something terrible is happening right that second.\n\nFor me it feels more like this ongoing buildup or background tension, if that makes sense. Like I can be worrying and feeling keyed up for a long time, and it might get worse depending on what I’m thinking about, but it doesn’t usually feel like a distinct episode that suddenly comes on super fast and then passes. At least, I haven’t really noticed it in that clear way.\n\nI guess I do sometimes feel very uncomfortable in my body when I’m anxious, but I’m not sure about the heart racing or shortness of breath part specifically. If it happens, I don’t think I’ve been labeling it as a separate episode. It’s more like… I’m just anxious and wound up, not like a sudden attack of fear.\n\nDoctor: Has this constant worry and tension made it harder for you to function in your daily life, like at work, in social situations, or with personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. I think that’s actually where it started feeling less like “I’m just stressed” and more like… okay, this is getting in the way of my life.\n\nAt work, I’m a computer support specialist, so there are always tickets coming in, and I used to be pretty good at moving through them. Not perfectly, but I could prioritize and get things done. Lately I’ve been falling behind because I’ll get stuck double-checking things that should be simple. Like, I’ll review a response before sending it, then review it again, then worry I misunderstood the issue, or that I didn’t document it clearly enough, or that I might have missed some obvious step. And then meanwhile the queue keeps building.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly, because coworkers have had to pick up some of the slack. No one has been cruel about it, but I can tell, and that makes me feel worse. I’ll see someone else take a ticket I should’ve handled, or they’ll gently ask if I’m okay, and then I start worrying about that too — like whether they think I’m unreliable.\n\nAnd outside work, it’s affected things I actually care about. I’ve skipped several dance nights because getting myself there just feels like too much when I’m already overwhelmed. And I’ve missed mentoring sessions too, which I feel really guilty about, because that’s something I usually value and want to show up for. It’s not that I don’t care; it’s more like by the time it comes around, I feel so wound up and overloaded that I just can’t make myself go.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble sleeping because your mind keeps racing or you’re unable to relax, even when you’re tired?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely.
