I’ve skipped several dance nights because getting myself there just feels like too much when I’m already overwhelmed. And I’ve missed mentoring sessions too, which I feel really guilty about, because that’s something I usually value and want to show up for. It’s not that I don’t care; it’s more like by the time it comes around, I feel so wound up and overloaded that I just can’t make myself go.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble sleeping because your mind keeps racing or you’re unable to relax, even when you’re tired?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. That’s been happening most nights, honestly. I’ll be tired — like, really tired — and I’ll get into bed thinking I should fall asleep quickly because my body feels worn out, but then as soon as it’s quiet my brain just starts going.\n\nIt’ll replay work tickets, like whether I handled something correctly or missed a step, or whether I documented something clearly enough. Then it jumps to loan notices or bills or something I saw in the mail, and I start thinking, did I forget a deadline, did I misunderstand something, what if there’s a problem I didn’t catch. And then somehow I’m mentally going through all these little details that I can’t do anything about at that moment anyway.\n\nI’ll try to tell myself, “You’re in bed, you can deal with it tomorrow,” but it doesn’t really settle. I keep feeling like I need to solve it before I can relax, except there’s nothing I can actually solve at 1 or 2 in the morning. A lot of nights I look at the clock and it’s suddenly 2 or 3 a.m., and then I get stressed about how little sleep I’m going to get, which makes it even harder.\n\nAnd even when I do finally sleep, it doesn’t feel very restful. I wake up feeling like I barely slept, like my mind was still half-running the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even outside of the times when you’re feeling anxious or wound up?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I wouldn’t say I feel hopeless in a clear way, like I’ve given up on everything or anything like that. It’s more hard for me to separate what’s “feeling down” from just being exhausted and overwhelmed all the time.\n\nLike, with things I usually enjoy, sometimes I don’t go or I don’t feel up for them, but I think that’s more because I’m tense or drained rather than because I don’t care about them anymore. If I actually imagine doing them on a day where I felt calmer, I think I would still want to. I haven’t really noticed a separate “down” feeling outside of that, at least not in a way I could clearly point to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a variety of things, even small ones, not just major life events or problems?\n\nPatient: Yes. And then it’ll shift again to money, like how I’m supposed to save enough or move forward in life if I’m already feeling behind.\n\nIt can be really small things too, not just major problems. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I’d say it’s more days than not. I don’t know if I notice it every single minute, but once it starts, it’s really hard to shut off. I’ll try to be logical with myself, like, “You already checked the spreadsheet,” or, “You already sent the email, there’s nothing else to do right now,” and for maybe a second that helps.

But then the thought just loops back around. Like, what if I missed something in the spreadsheet, or what if the email sounded wrong, or what if I sent it to the wrong person, or what if someone reads it and thinks I’m incompetent. It’s like one “what if” branches into five more before I can even catch up with it.

And I do try to stop it. I’ll reread things, or mentally review what I did, or tell myself I’m being unreasonable, but it doesn’t really feel under control. It’s more like I’m trying to pull myself back over and over again, and sometimes I can for a little while, but then my mind grabs onto the same thing or finds a new thing to worry about. So yes, it does feel pretty hard to control.

Honestly, that’s part of what feels so frustrating about it, because it doesn’t always need a trigger. I can be completely by myself, with nothing actively happening, and I’ll still feel like my body is waiting for something bad to happen.\n\nLike I’ll just be sitting at my desk, or at home, or even trying to do something that should be enjoyable, and there’s this wound-up feeling in me. My shoulders get tight, and I feel like I’m bracing, almost like I’m preparing for the next problem before I even know what it is.\n\nAnd with dance night, too — even though I want to relax and enjoy it, I’ll notice I’m still tense inside. It just stays on alert.\n\nDoctor: How often do these anxious feelings and the physical tension happen—like, are they present most days, or do they come and go more sporadically?\n\nPatient: It’s most days. I wish it were more like it came and went in a clear way, but it feels pretty constant lately.\n\nI mean, it may get louder or quieter depending on what I’m doing, but the baseline is usually there — like I’m wound up, or my body is bracing for the next bad thing. That’s actually one of the parts that bothers me the most, because I can sometimes see that the worry isn’t really helping, but I still can’t just… shut it off.\n\nLike at work, I’ll tell myself, “Okay, I already checked the spreadsheet,” or “I already sent that email, it’s done,” but then my brain keeps circling back to it anyway. I’ll start wondering, what if I missed something, what if I sent it to the wrong person, what if there was an attachment issue, what if someone reads it the wrong way. And then it’s like one “what if” turns into five more before I can pull myself back.\n\nEven when there isn’t one clear thing to worry about, my mind kind of searches for something. I can know logically that I did the task, or checked it, or handled it, but the thoughts keep looping like they don’t believe me. It’s exhausting because I’ll try to reassure myself, but it only works for a little bit, if at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced sudden episodes where you feel an intense wave of fear or discomfort that comes on very quickly, like your heart starts racing, you feel short of breath, or like something terrible is going to happen right then and there?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d describe it that way. I mean, I definitely get anxious and tense, and sometimes it can feel really uncomfortable, but I don’t know that I’ve had those sudden, out-of-nowhere waves where it’s like everything spikes all at once and I feel like something terrible is happening right that second.\n\nFor me it feels more like this ongoing buildup or background tension, if that makes sense. Like I can be worrying and feeling keyed up for a long time, and it might get worse depending on what I’m thinking about, but it doesn’t usually feel like a distinct episode that suddenly comes on super fast and then passes. At least, I haven’t really noticed it in that clear way.\n\nI guess I do sometimes feel very uncomfortable in my body when I’m anxious, but I’m not sure about the heart racing or shortness of breath part specifically. If it happens, I don’t think I’ve been labeling it as a separate episode. It’s more like… I’m just anxious and wound up, not like a sudden attack of fear.\n\nDoctor: Has this constant worry and tension made it harder for you to function in your daily life, like at work, in social situations, or with personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. I think that’s actually where it started feeling less like “I’m just stressed” and more like… okay, this is getting in the way of my life.\n\nAt work, I’m a computer support specialist, so there are always tickets coming in, and I used to be pretty good at moving through them. Not perfectly, but I could prioritize and get things done. Lately I’ve been falling behind because I’ll get stuck double-checking things that should be simple. Like, I’ll review a response before sending it, then review it again, then worry I misunderstood the issue, or that I didn’t document it clearly enough, or that I might have missed some obvious step. And then meanwhile the queue keeps building.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly, because coworkers have had to pick up some of the slack. No one has been cruel about it, but I can tell, and that makes me feel worse. I’ll see someone else take a ticket I should’ve handled, or they’ll gently ask if I’m okay, and then I start worrying about that too — like whether they think I’m unreliable.\n\nAnd outside work, it’s affected things I actually care about. I’ve skipped several dance nights because getting myself there just feels like too much when I’m already overwhelmed. And I’ve missed mentoring sessions too, which I feel really guilty about, because that’s something I usually value and want to show up for. It’s not that I don’t care; it’s more like by the time it comes around, I feel so wound up and overloaded that I just can’t make myself go.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble sleeping because your mind keeps racing or you’re unable to relax, even when you’re tired?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. That’s been happening most nights, honestly. I’ll be tired — like, really tired — and I’ll get into bed thinking I should fall asleep quickly because my body feels worn out, but then as soon as it’s quiet my brain just starts going.\n\nIt’ll replay work tickets, like whether I handled something correctly or missed a step, or whether I documented something clearly enough. Then it jumps to loan notices or bills or something I saw in the mail, and I start thinking, did I forget a deadline, did I misunderstand something, what if there’s a problem I didn’t catch. And then somehow I’m mentally going through all these little details that I can’t do anything about at that moment anyway.\n\nI’ll try to tell myself, “You’re in bed, you can deal with it tomorrow,” but it doesn’t really settle. I keep feeling like I need to solve it before I can relax, except there’s nothing I can actually solve at 1 or 2 in the morning. A lot of nights I look at the clock and it’s suddenly 2 or 3 a.m., and then I get stressed about how little sleep I’m going to get, which makes it even harder.\n\nAnd even when I do finally sleep, it doesn’t feel very restful. I wake up feeling like I barely slept, like my mind was still half-running the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even outside of the times when you’re feeling anxious or wound up?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I wouldn’t say I feel hopeless in a clear way, like I’ve given up on everything or anything like that. It’s more hard for me to separate what’s “feeling down” from just being exhausted and overwhelmed all the time.\n\nLike, with things I usually enjoy, sometimes I don’t go or I don’t feel up for them, but I think that’s more because I’m tense or drained rather than because I don’t care about them anymore. If I actually imagine doing them on a day where I felt calmer, I think I would still want to. So I don’t know that it’s a loss of interest exactly.\n\nI do get discouraged with myself, especially when I feel like I’m not keeping up or I’m disappointing people, but it feels tied to being wound up and stressed rather than this separate low mood that’s there on its own. I haven’t really noticed a separate “down” feeling outside of that, at least not in a way I could clearly point to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a variety of things, even small ones, not just major life events or problems?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s not just one big thing anymore, which is part of why it feels so exhausting. At first I could kind of tell myself, okay, I’m worried about the condo situation, that’s stressful, that makes sense. But now it’s like my mind doesn’t stay there — it jumps around to anything it can grab onto.\n\nI’ll worry that my cousin is upset with me because of something small, like if a text sounded a little short or if I didn’t respond the “right” way. Then five minutes later I’m worrying about work, like whether I’m going to make some mistake on a support call or misunderstand what someone is asking for. And then it’ll shift again to money, like how I’m supposed to save enough or move forward in life if I’m already feeling behind.\n\nIt can be really small things too, not just major problems. I’ll take something ordinary and then my brain turns it into this whole chain of “what ifs.” It feels like I’m constantly scanning for what might go wrong next, even when there isn’t one clear emergency in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced sudden, intense episodes of fear where you felt like something terrible was happening, such as your heart racing, feeling dizzy, or short of breath, even when there was no real danger?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that really sudden, out-of-nowhere way you’re describing. I mean, I definitely get anxious and worked up, and sometimes it can feel intense, but it’s usually more like it builds because I’m thinking about something or worrying through a situation.\n\nI don’t really remember having an episode where, all of a sudden, I felt like I was in immediate danger for no clear reason, like “something terrible is happening right now.” I might feel tense or uneasy, but I don’t think I’ve had that kind of abrupt wave of fear with the heart racing or dizziness or not being able to breathe in the way people describe.\n\nI guess if anything, my fear tends to be more drawn out and tied to my thoughts, not like a sudden attack that peaks quickly. At least, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your anxiety and worry happen on more days than not, and that it’s hard for you to control it, even when you try?\n\nPatient: Yes, I’d say it’s more days than not.
